border-collapse:collapse; border-spacing:0px; border:none; width:100%; margin:0px; font-size:100%; clear:none; float:none"><tbody><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-above" style="font-size:100%; padding-left:2em; background-color:#eee"><span style="font-size:100%;">Episcopal succession</span></th></tr><tr><th colspan="2" class="infobox-header"><div style="background:#f2f2f2; border-bottom: 3px solid #800080; border-top: 3px solid #800080">Bishops consecrated by Pope Sixtus IV as principal consecrator</div></th></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pierre_Engelpert&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Pierre Engelpert (page does not exist)">Pierre Engelpert</a></th><td class="infobox-data">25 March 1477</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Georg_Hessler" class="mw-redirect" title="Georg Hessler">Georg Hessler</a></th><td class="infobox-data">13 February 1480</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/wiki/Giuliano_della_Rovere" class="mw-redirect" title="Giuliano della Rovere">Giuliano della Rovere</a></th><td class="infobox-data">1481</td></tr><tr><th scope="row" class="infobox-label"><a href="/w/index.php?title=Matthias_Scheit&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Matthias Scheit (page does not exist)">Matthias Scheit</a></th><td class="infobox-data">31 December 1481</td></tr></tbody></table></td></tr></tbody></table> 
<p><b>Pope Sixtus IV</b> (<a href="/wiki/Italian_language" title="Italian language">Italian</a>: <i lang="it">Sisto IV</i>: 21 July 1414&#160;– 12 August 1484), born <b>Francesco della Rovere</b>, was head of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a> and ruler of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a> from 9 August 1471 to his death in August 1484. His accomplishments as <a href="/wiki/Pope" title="Pope">pope</a> included the construction of the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a> and the creation of the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Library" title="Vatican Library">Vatican Library</a>. A patron of the arts, he brought together the group of artists who ushered the Early <a href="/wiki/Renaissance" title="Renaissance">Renaissance</a> into <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> with the first masterpieces of the city's new artistic age.
</p><p>Sixtus founded the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Spanish Inquisition</a> through the bull <i>Exigit sincerae devotionis affectus</i> (1478), and he annulled the decrees of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Constance" title="Council of Constance">Council of Constance</a>. He was noted for his nepotism and was personally involved in the infamous <a href="/wiki/Pazzi_conspiracy" title="Pazzi conspiracy">Pazzi conspiracy</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-1">&#91;1&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Early_life">Early life</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Early life">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Francesco was born to a family of modest means from <a href="/wiki/Liguria" title="Liguria">Liguria</a>, <a href="/wiki/Italy" title="Italy">Italy</a>, the son of Leonardo <a href="/wiki/Della_Rovere" title="Della Rovere">della Rovere</a> and Luchina Monleoni. He was born in <a href="/wiki/Celle_Ligure" title="Celle Ligure">Celle Ligure</a>, a town near <a href="/wiki/Savona" title="Savona">Savona</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Miranda_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Miranda-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a young man, Della Rovere joined the <a href="/wiki/Franciscans" title="Franciscans">Franciscan Order</a>, an unlikely choice for a political career, and his intellectual qualities were revealed while he was studying <a href="/wiki/Philosophy" title="Philosophy">philosophy</a> and <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a> at the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Pavia" title="University of Pavia">University of Pavia</a>. He went on to lecture at Padua and many other Italian universities.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In 1464, Della Rovere was elected <a href="/wiki/Minister_General_of_the_Franciscan_order" class="mw-redirect" title="Minister General of the Franciscan order">Minister General of the Franciscan order</a> at the age of 50. In 1467, he was appointed <a href="/wiki/Cardinal_(Catholicism)" class="mw-redirect" title="Cardinal (Catholicism)">Cardinal</a> by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Paul_II" title="Pope Paul II">Pope Paul II</a> with the <a href="/wiki/Titular_church" title="Titular church">titular church</a> being the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Pietro_in_Vincoli" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of San Pietro in Vincoli">Basilica of San Pietro in Vincoli</a>.
</p><p>Before his papal election, Cardinal della Rovere was renowned for his unworldliness and had written learned treatises, including <i>On the Blood of Christ</i> and <i>On the Power of God</i>.<sup id="cite_ref-4" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His reputation for piety was one of the deciding factors that prompted the <a href="/wiki/College_of_Cardinals" title="College of Cardinals">College of Cardinals</a> to elect him Pope upon the unexpected death of Paul II at the age of fifty-four.<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Papacy">Papacy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: Papacy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/1471_papal_conclave" title="1471 papal conclave">1471 papal conclave</a></div>
<p>Upon being elected Pope, Della Rovere adopted the name Sixtus, which had not been used since the 5th century. One of his first acts was to declare a renewed <a href="/wiki/Crusade" class="mw-redirect" title="Crusade">crusade</a> against the <a href="/wiki/Ottoman_Turks" title="Ottoman Turks">Ottoman Turks</a> in <a href="/wiki/Smyrna" title="Smyrna">Smyrna</a>. However, after the conquest of Smyrna, the fleet disbanded.<sup id="cite_ref-Palazzo_6-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Palazzo-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> Some fruitless attempts were made towards unification with the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Greek Church</a>. For the remainder of his pontificate, Sixtus turned to temporal issues and dynastic considerations.
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Nepotism">Nepotism</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Nepotism">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg/220px-Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg/330px-Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/5/56/Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg/440px-Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg 2x" data-file-width="2024" data-file-height="2407" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC_001.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div>Pope Sixtus IV appoints Platina as Prefect of the Library, by <a href="/wiki/Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC" title="Melozzo da Forlì">Melozzo da Forlì</a>, accompanied by his relatives</div></div></div>
<p>Sixtus IV sought to strengthen his position by surrounding himself with relatives and friends. In the fresco by <a href="/wiki/Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC" title="Melozzo da Forlì">Melozzo da Forlì</a>, he is accompanied by his <a href="/wiki/Della_Rovere" title="Della Rovere">Della Rovere</a> and <a href="/wiki/Riario" title="Riario">Riario</a> nephews, not all of whom were made cardinals; the <a href="/wiki/Protonotary_apostolic" title="Protonotary apostolic">protonotary apostolic</a> <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Riario" title="Pietro Riario">Pietro Riario</a> (on his right), the future Pope <a href="/wiki/Julius_II" class="mw-redirect" title="Julius II">Julius II</a>/ Giuliano Della Rovere standing before him; and <a href="/wiki/Girolamo_Riario" title="Girolamo Riario">Girolamo Riario</a> and <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_della_Rovere" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni della Rovere">Giovanni della Rovere</a>, behind the kneeling <a href="/wiki/Bartolomeo_Platina" title="Bartolomeo Platina">Platina</a>, author of the first <a href="/wiki/Humanism" title="Humanism">humanist</a> history of the popes.<sup id="cite_ref-Morris_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morris-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>His nephew, Pietro Riario, possibly also benefited from his alleged nepotism. He was successively promoted to be a cardinal, the bishop of Florence, the Patriarch of Constantinople and given some 45 additional <a href="/wiki/Benefice" title="Benefice">benefices</a>. Pietro became one of the richest men in <a href="/wiki/Rome" title="Rome">Rome</a> and was entrusted with Pope Sixtus IV's foreign policy, in addition to being given an unofficial post as the de facto ruler of Rome. He reportedly spent 200,000 <a href="/wiki/Ducat" title="Ducat">gold ducats</a> on foodstuffs and festivities during two years in office.<sup id="cite_ref-8" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-8">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Pietro died prematurely in 1474.<sup id="cite_ref-9" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-9">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> Chroniclers of his life seem to regard his death as unnatural and thus connect his alleged grandiose spending habits and the impression they left on his contemporaries as causal.<sup id="cite_ref-10" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-10">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Criticisms of <a href="/wiki/Pietro_Riario" title="Pietro Riario">Pietro</a>'s meteoric rise were not constrained to the charge of benefiting from nepotism as Sixtus IV's nephew, nor to allegations of possibly having been Sixtus IV's illegitimate son. Indeed, Pietro and his brother Girolamo Riario were alleged to have been lovers of Sixtus IV in polemics against the latter. According to the later published chronicle of the Italian historian <a href="/wiki/Stefano_Infessura" title="Stefano Infessura">Stefano Infessura</a>, <i>Diary of the City of Rome</i>, Sixtus was a "lover of boys and a sodomite" (<a href="/wiki/Latin_language" class="mw-redirect" title="Latin language">Latin</a>: <i lang="la">puerorum amator et sodomita</i>) awarding benefices and bishoprics in return for sexual favours and nominating a number of young men as cardinals.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> Sexualised polemics, in truth concerned with politics and not the sexual lives of their victims, were not uncommon during this time; but as <a href="/wiki/Ulrich_Pfisterer_(art_historian)" title="Ulrich Pfisterer (art historian)">Pfisterer</a> notes "the novel flood of accusations of sodomy against a pope" and "true flood of corresponding lampoons, reviling poems, and fictional epitaphs" following his death are at the very least evidence for his contemporaries' opinions about the promotions of these young men.<sup id="cite_ref-12" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-12">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The secular fortunes of the Della Rovere family began when Sixtus invested his nephew <a href="/wiki/Giovanni_della_Rovere" class="mw-redirect" title="Giovanni della Rovere">Giovanni</a> with the lordship of <a href="/wiki/Senigallia" title="Senigallia">Senigallia</a> and arranged his marriage to the daughter of <a href="/wiki/Federico_III_da_Montefeltro" class="mw-redirect" title="Federico III da Montefeltro">Federico III da Montefeltro</a>, duke of <a href="/wiki/Urbino" title="Urbino">Urbino</a>; from that union came a line of Della Rovere dukes of Urbino that lasted until the line expired, in 1631.<sup id="cite_ref-13" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-13">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup> Six of the thirty-four cardinals that he created were his nephews.<sup id="cite_ref-14" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-14">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In his territorial aggrandizement of the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>, his niece's son, Cardinal <a href="/wiki/Raffaele_Riario" title="Raffaele Riario">Raffaele Riario</a> (for whom the <a href="/wiki/Palazzo_della_Cancelleria" title="Palazzo della Cancelleria">Palazzo della Cancelleria</a> was constructed)<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> was suspected of colluding in the failed <a href="/wiki/Pazzi_conspiracy" title="Pazzi conspiracy">Pazzi conspiracy</a> of 1478 to assassinate both <a href="/wiki/Lorenzo_de%27_Medici" title="Lorenzo de&#39; Medici">Lorenzo de' Medici</a> and his brother <a href="/wiki/Giuliano_di_Piero_de%27_Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Giuliano di Piero de&#39; Medici">Giuliano</a> and replace them in <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a> with Sixtus IV's other nephew, <a href="/wiki/Riario" title="Riario">Girolamo Riario</a>. <a href="/wiki/Francesco_Salviati_(archbishop)" class="mw-redirect" title="Francesco Salviati (archbishop)">Francesco Salviati</a>, <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Pisa" class="mw-redirect" title="Archbishop of Pisa">Archbishop of Pisa</a> and a main organizer of the plot, was hanged on the walls of the Florentine <a href="/wiki/Palazzo_della_Signoria" class="mw-redirect" title="Palazzo della Signoria">Palazzo della Signoria</a>. Sixtus IV replied with an <a href="/wiki/Interdict_(Catholic_canon_law)" class="mw-redirect" title="Interdict (Catholic canon law)">interdict</a> and two years of war with Florence.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>However, Infessura had partisan allegiances to the <a href="/wiki/Colonna" class="mw-redirect" title="Colonna">Colonna</a> and so is not considered to be always reliable or impartial.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> The English churchman and <a href="/wiki/Protestant" class="mw-redirect" title="Protestant">Protestant</a> polemicist <a href="/wiki/John_Bale" title="John Bale">John Bale</a>, writing a century later, attributed to Sixtus "the authorisation to practice <a href="/wiki/Sodomy" title="Sodomy">sodomy</a> during periods of warm weather" to the "Cardinal of Santa Lucia".<sup id="cite_ref-16" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-16">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> This prompted the noted historian of the Catholic Church, <a href="/wiki/Ludwig_von_Pastor" title="Ludwig von Pastor">Ludwig von Pastor</a>, to issue a firm rebuttal.<sup id="cite_ref-17" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-17">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Foreign_policy">Foreign policy</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: Foreign policy">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Sixtus continued a dispute with King <a href="/wiki/Louis_XI_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XI of France">Louis XI of France</a>, who upheld the <a href="/wiki/Pragmatic_Sanction_of_Bourges" title="Pragmatic Sanction of Bourges">Pragmatic Sanction of Bourges</a> (1438), which held that papal decrees needed royal assent before they could be promulgated in France.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler_3-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup> That was a cornerstone of the privileges claimed for the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church_in_France" title="Catholic Church in France">Gallican Church</a> and could never be shifted as long as Louis XI manoeuvred to replace King <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_I_of_Naples" title="Ferdinand I of Naples">Ferdinand I of Naples</a> with a French prince. Louis was thus in conflict with the papacy, and Sixtus could not permit it.
</p><p>On 1 November 1478, Sixtus published the <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> <i>Exigit Sincerae Devotionis Affectus</i> through which the <a href="/wiki/Spanish_Inquisition" title="Spanish Inquisition">Spanish Inquisition</a> was established in the <a href="/wiki/Kingdom_of_Castile" title="Kingdom of Castile">Kingdom of Castile</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECostigan201015_18-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECostigan201015-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> Sixtus consented under political pressure from <a href="/wiki/Ferdinand_II_of_Aragon" title="Ferdinand II of Aragon">Ferdinand of Aragon</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTECostigan201015_18-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTECostigan201015-18">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup> who threatened to withhold military support from his kingdom of <a href="/wiki/Sicily" title="Sicily">Sicily</a>. Nevertheless, Sixtus IV quarrelled over protocol and prerogatives of jurisdiction; he was unhappy with the excesses of the Inquisition and condemned the most flagrant abuses in 1482.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEKamen199749_19-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEKamen199749-19">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>As a temporal prince who constructed stout fortresses in the <a href="/wiki/Papal_States" title="Papal States">Papal States</a>, he encouraged the <a href="/wiki/Republic_of_Venice" title="Republic of Venice">Venetians</a> to attack <a href="/wiki/War_of_Ferrara" title="War of Ferrara">Ferrara</a>, which he wished to obtain for another nephew. <a href="/wiki/Ercole_I_d%27Este" class="mw-redirect" title="Ercole I d&#39;Este">Ercole I d'Este</a>, <a href="/wiki/Duke_of_Ferrara" class="mw-redirect" title="Duke of Ferrara">Duke of Ferrara</a>, was allied with the <a href="/wiki/Sforza" class="mw-redirect" title="Sforza">Sforzas</a> of <a href="/wiki/Milan" title="Milan">Milan</a>, the <a href="/wiki/Medici" class="mw-redirect" title="Medici">Medicis</a> of <a href="/wiki/Florence" title="Florence">Florence</a> along with the <a href="/wiki/King_of_Naples" class="mw-redirect" title="King of Naples">King of Naples</a>, normally a hereditary ally and champion of the papacy. The angered Italian princes allied to force Sixtus IV to make peace to his great annoyance.<sup id="cite_ref-Butler_3-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Butler-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>For refusing to desist from the very hostilities that he himself had instigated and for being a dangerous rival to Della Rovere dynastic ambitions in the <a href="/wiki/Marche" title="Marche">Marche</a>, Sixtus placed Venice under <a href="/wiki/Interdict" title="Interdict">interdict</a> in 1483. He also lined the coffers of the state by unscrupulously selling high offices and privileges.<sup id="cite_ref-Palazzo_6-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Palazzo-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In ecclesiastical affairs, Sixtus promoted the dogma of the <a href="/wiki/Immaculate_Conception_of_Mary" class="mw-redirect" title="Immaculate Conception of Mary">Immaculate Conception</a>, which had been confirmed at the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Basle" class="mw-redirect" title="Council of Basle">Council of Basle</a> in 1439,<sup id="cite_ref-Palazzo_6-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Palazzo-6">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> and he designated 8 December as its feastday. In 1476, he issued the apostolic constitution <i>Cum Praeexcelsa</i>, establishing a Mass and Office for the feast. He formally annulled the decrees of the <a href="/wiki/Council_of_Constance" title="Council of Constance">Council of Constance</a> in 1478.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Slavery">Slavery</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Slavery">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The two papal bulls issued by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Nicholas_V" title="Pope Nicholas V">Pope Nicholas V</a>, <i><a href="/wiki/Dum_Diversas" title="Dum Diversas">Dum Diversas</a></i> of 1452 and <i><a href="/wiki/Romanus_Pontifex" title="Romanus Pontifex">Romanus Pontifex</a></i> of 1455, had effectively given the Portuguese the rights to acquire slaves along the African Coast by force or trade. Those concessions were confirmed by Sixtus in his own bull, <i><a href="/wiki/Aeterni_regis" title="Aeterni regis">Aeterni regis</a></i>, of 21 June 1481.<sup id="cite_ref-21" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-21">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Arguably the "ideology of conquest" expounded in those texts became the means by which commerce and conversion were facilitated.<sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In November 1476, Isabel and Fernando ordered an investigation into rights of conquest in the Canary Islands, and in the spring of 1478, they sent Juan Rejon with sixty soldiers and thirty cavalry to the Grand Canary, where the natives retreated inland.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> Sixtus's earlier threats to excommunicate all captains or pirates who enslaved Christians in the bull <i>Regimini Gregis</i> of 1476 could have been intended to emphasise the need to convert the natives of the <a href="/wiki/Canary_Islands" title="Canary Islands">Canary Islands</a> and <a href="/wiki/Guinea" title="Guinea">Guinea</a> and establish a clear difference in status between those who had converted and those who resisted.<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup> The ecclesiastical penalties were directed towards those who were enslaving the recent converts.<sup id="cite_ref-24" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-24">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Princely_patronage">Princely patronage</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Princely patronage">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<div class="thumb tleft"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_(cropped).jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_%28cropped%29.jpg/220px-Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_%28cropped%29.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="262" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_%28cropped%29.jpg/330px-Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_%28cropped%29.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/9/9b/Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_%28cropped%29.jpg/440px-Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_%28cropped%29.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1727" data-file-height="2055" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Tizian_Portrait_Papst_Sixtus_IV_ca._1545-46_Uffizien_Florenz-01_(cropped).jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><i><a href="/wiki/Portrait_of_Pope_Sixtus_IV" title="Portrait of Pope Sixtus IV">Pope Sixtus IV</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Titian" title="Titian">Titian</a> and his studio, oil on panel,  109.5&#160;×&#160;87&#160;cm, <a href="/wiki/Uffizi" title="Uffizi">Uffizi</a></div></div></div>
<p>As a civic patron in Rome, even the anti-papal chronicler Stefano Infessura agreed that Sixtus should be admired. The dedicatory inscription in the fresco by <a href="/wiki/Melozzo_da_Forl%C3%AC" title="Melozzo da Forlì">Melozzo da Forlì</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Palace" class="mw-redirect" title="Vatican Palace">Vatican Palace</a> records: "You gave your city temples, streets, squares, fortifications, bridges and restored the <a href="/wiki/Acqua_Vergine" title="Acqua Vergine">Acqua Vergine</a> as far as the <a href="/wiki/Trevi_Fountain" title="Trevi Fountain">Trevi</a>..."
</p><p>In addition to restoring the aqueduct that provided Rome an alternative to the river water, which had made the city famously unhealthy, he restored or rebuilt over 30 of Rome's dilapidated churches such as <a href="/wiki/San_Vitale,_Rome" title="San Vitale, Rome">San Vitale</a> (1475) and <a href="/wiki/Santa_Maria_del_Popolo" title="Santa Maria del Popolo">Santa Maria del Popolo</a>, and he added seven new ones. The <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Chapel" title="Sistine Chapel">Sistine Chapel</a> was sponsored by Sixtus IV, as was the <i>Ponte Sisto</i>,<sup id="cite_ref-Morris_7-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morris-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Sistine_Bridge" class="mw-redirect" title="Sistine Bridge">Sistine Bridge</a> (the first new bridge across the <a href="/wiki/Tiber" title="Tiber">Tiber</a> since Antiquity), and the building of <i>Via Sistina</i> (later named <i>Borgo Sant'Angelo</i>), a road leading from <a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a> to Saint Peter.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>All of that was done to facilitate the integration of the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Hill" title="Vatican Hill">Vatican Hill</a> and <a href="/wiki/Borgo_(rione_of_Rome)" title="Borgo (rione of Rome)">Borgo</a> with the heart of Old Rome. That was part of a broader scheme of <a href="/wiki/Urbanization" title="Urbanization">urbanization</a> carried out under Sixtus IV, who swept the long-established markets from the <a href="/wiki/Capitoline_Hill" title="Capitoline Hill">Campidoglio</a> in 1477 and decreed in a bull of 1480 the widening of streets and the first post-Roman paving, the removal of porticoes and other post-classical impediments to free public passage.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<div class="thumb tright"><div class="thumbinner" style="width:222px;"><a href="/wiki/File:Ponte_Sisto,_Rome.jpg" class="image"><img alt="" src="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Ponte_Sisto%2C_Rome.jpg/220px-Ponte_Sisto%2C_Rome.jpg" decoding="async" width="220" height="165" class="thumbimage" srcset="//upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Ponte_Sisto%2C_Rome.jpg/330px-Ponte_Sisto%2C_Rome.jpg 1.5x, //upload.wikimedia.org/wikipedia/commons/thumb/7/7a/Ponte_Sisto%2C_Rome.jpg/440px-Ponte_Sisto%2C_Rome.jpg 2x" data-file-width="1024" data-file-height="768" /></a>  <div class="thumbcaption"><div class="magnify"><a href="/wiki/File:Ponte_Sisto,_Rome.jpg" class="internal" title="Enlarge"></a></div><a href="/wiki/Ponte_Sisto" title="Ponte Sisto">Ponte Sisto</a>, the first bridge built at Rome since the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Empire" title="Roman Empire">Roman Empire</a></div></div></div>
<p>At the beginning of his papacy, in 1471, Sixtus had donated several historically important Roman sculptures that founded a papal collection of art, which would eventually develop into the collections of the <a href="/wiki/Capitoline_Museums" title="Capitoline Museums">Capitoline Museums</a>. He also refounded, enriched and enlarged the <a href="/wiki/Vatican_Library" title="Vatican Library">Vatican Library</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-Morris_7-2" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Morris-7">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> He had <a href="/wiki/Regiomontanus" title="Regiomontanus">Regiomontanus</a> attempt the first sanctioned reorganisation of the <a href="/wiki/Julian_calendar" title="Julian calendar">Julian calendar</a> and increased the size and prestige of the papal chapel choir, bringing singers and some prominent composers (<a href="/wiki/Gaspar_van_Weerbeke" title="Gaspar van Weerbeke">Gaspar van Weerbeke</a>, <a href="/wiki/Marbrianus_de_Orto" title="Marbrianus de Orto">Marbrianus de Orto</a> and <a href="/w/index.php?title=Bertrandus_Vaqueras&amp;action=edit&amp;redlink=1" class="new" title="Bertrandus Vaqueras (page does not exist)">Bertrandus Vaqueras</a>) to Rome from the north.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>In addition to being a patron of the arts, Sixtus was a patron of the sciences. Before he became pope, he had spent time at the very liberal and cosmopolitan <a href="/wiki/University_of_Padua" title="University of Padua">University of Padua</a>, which maintained considerable independence from the Church and had a very international character.<sup id="cite_ref-25" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-25">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup><sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>As Pope, he issued a <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> allowing local bishops to give the bodies of executed criminals and unidentified corpses to physicians and artists for dissection. It was that access to corpses which allowed the anatomist <a href="/wiki/Vesalius" class="mw-redirect" title="Vesalius">Vesalius</a>, along with <a href="/wiki/Titian" title="Titian">Titian</a>'s pupil <a href="/wiki/Jan_Van_Calcar" class="mw-redirect" title="Jan Van Calcar">Jan Stephen van Calcar</a>, to complete the revolutionary medical/anatomical text <i><a href="/wiki/De_humani_corporis_fabrica" class="mw-redirect" title="De humani corporis fabrica">De humani corporis fabrica</a></i>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (October 2021)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Other_activities">Other activities</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Other activities">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Consistories">Consistories</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Consistories">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cardinals_created_by_Sixtus_IV" title="Cardinals created by Sixtus IV">Cardinals created by Sixtus IV</a></div>
<p>The Pope created 34 cardinals in eight consistories held during his reign, among them three nephews, one grandnephew and one other relative, thus continuing the practice of nepotism that he and his successors would engage in during this period.
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Canonizations_and_beatifications">Canonizations and beatifications</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Canonizations and beatifications">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>Sixtus IV named seven new saints, with the most notable being <a href="/wiki/Bonaventure" title="Bonaventure">Bonaventure</a> (1482); he also beatified one person, John Buoni (1483).
</p>
<h4><span class="mw-headline" id="Uppsala_University">Uppsala University</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Uppsala University">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h4>
<p>In 1477, Sixtus IV issued a <a href="/wiki/Papal_bull" title="Papal bull">papal bull</a> authorizing the creation of <a href="/wiki/Uppsala_University" title="Uppsala University">Uppsala University</a> – the first university in <a href="/wiki/Sweden" title="Sweden">Sweden</a> and in the whole of <a href="/wiki/Scandinavia" title="Scandinavia">Scandinavia</a>. The choice of this location for the university derived from the fact that the <a href="/wiki/Archbishop_of_Uppsala" title="Archbishop of Uppsala">archbishopric of Uppsala</a> had been one of the most important <a href="/wiki/Episcopal_See" class="mw-redirect" title="Episcopal See">sees</a> in <a href="/wiki/Sweden_proper" title="Sweden proper">Sweden proper</a> since Christianity first spread to this region in the ninth century, as well as Uppsala being long-standing hub for regional trade. Uppsala's bull, which granted the university its corporate rights, established a number of provisions. Among the most important of these was that the university was officially given the same freedoms and privileges as the <a href="/wiki/University_of_Bologna" title="University of Bologna">University of Bologna</a>. This included the right to establish the four traditional faculties of <a href="/wiki/Theology" title="Theology">theology</a>, law (<a href="/wiki/Canon_law_(Catholic_Church)" class="mw-redirect" title="Canon law (Catholic Church)">Canon Law</a> and <a href="/wiki/Roman_law" title="Roman law">Roman law</a>), medicine, and philosophy, and to award the bachelor's, master's, licentiate, and doctoral degrees. The archbishop of Uppsala was also named as the university's <a href="/wiki/Chancellor_(education)" title="Chancellor (education)">Chancellor</a>, and was charged with maintaining the rights and privileges of the university and its members.<sup id="cite_ref-26" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-26">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup> This act of Sixtus IV had a profound long-term effect on the society and culture of Sweden, an effect which continues up to the present.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Death">Death</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Death">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1097763485">.mw-parser-output .ambox{border:1px solid #a2a9b1;border-left:10px solid #36c;background-color:#fbfbfb;box-sizing:border-box}.mw-parser-output ambox-Refimprove" role="presentation"><tbody><tr><td class="mbox-image"><div class="mbox-image-div"><a href="/wiki/File:Question_book-new.svg" class="image"><img alt="" class="mbox-text-span">This section <b>needs additional citations for <a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Verifiability" title="Wikipedia:Verifiability">verification</a></b>.<span class="hide-when-compact"> Please help <a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit">improve this article</a> by <a href="/wiki/Help:Referencing_for_beginners" title="Help:Referencing for beginners">adding citations to reliable sources</a>. Unsourced material may be challenged and removed.<br /><small><span class="plainlinks"><i>Find sources:</i>&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?as_eq=wikipedia&amp;q=%22Pope+Sixtus+IV%22">"Pope Sixtus IV"</a>&#160;–&#160;<a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?tbm=nws&amp;q=%22Pope+Sixtus+IV%22+-wikipedia&amp;tbs=ar:1">news</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?&amp;q=%22Pope+Sixtus+IV%22&amp;tbs=bkt:s&amp;tbm=bks">newspapers</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//www.google.com/search?tbs=bks:1&amp;q=%22Pope+Sixtus+IV%22+-wikipedia">books</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="//scholar.google.com/scholar?q=%22Pope+Sixtus+IV%22">scholar</a>&#160;<b>·</b> <a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://www.jstor.org/action/doBasicSearch?Query=%22Pope+Sixtus+IV%22&amp;acc=on&amp;wc=on">JSTOR</a></span></small></span>  <span class="date-container"><i>(<span class="date">October 2021</span>)</i></span><span class="hide-when-compact"><i> (<small><a href="/wiki/Help:Maintenance_template_removal" title="Help:Maintenance template removal">Learn how and when to remove this template message</a></small>)</i></span></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>Sixtus IV became ill on 8 August 1484; this illness worsened on 10 August while the pope was attending an event in Rome. He felt unwell that evening and was forced to cancel a meeting he was to hold with his cardinals the following morning. The Pope grew weaker during the night of 11 August and he was unable to sleep. Sixtus IV died the following evening – 12 August.<sup id="cite_ref-27" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-27">&#91;27&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The envoy of the Medici family summed up Sixtus' reign in the announcement to his master, "Today at 5 o'clock His Holiness Sixtus IV departed this life – may God forgive him!"<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;28&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>Pope Sixtus's tomb was destroyed in the <a href="/wiki/Sack_of_Rome_(1527)" title="Sack of Rome (1527)">Sack of Rome in 1527</a>. Today, his remains, along with the remains of his nephew Pope Julius II (Giuliano della Rovere), are interred in St. Peter's Basilica, in the floor in front of the monument to Pope Clement X. A marble tombstone marks the site.
</p><p>His bronze funerary monument, now in the basement Treasury of <a href="/wiki/St._Peter%27s_Basilica" title="St. Peter&#39;s Basilica">St. Peter's Basilica</a>, made like a giant casket of goldsmith's work, is by <a href="/wiki/Antonio_del_Pollaiuolo" title="Antonio del Pollaiuolo">Antonio del Pollaiuolo</a>. The top of the casket is a lifelike depiction of the Pope lying in state. Around the sides are <a href="/wiki/Bas-relief" class="mw-redirect" title="Bas-relief">bas-relief</a> panels depicting allegorical female figures representing Grammar, Rhetoric, Arithmetic, Geometry, Music, Painting, Astronomy, Philosophy, and Theology—the classical <a href="/wiki/Liberal_arts_education" title="Liberal arts education">liberal arts</a>, with the addition of painting and theology. Each figure incorporates the oak tree ("rovere" in Italian), symbol of Sixtus IV. The overall program of the panels, their beauty, complex symbolism, classical references and their relative arrangement are compelling and comprehensive illustrations of the Renaissance worldview. None of them actually states how he died.
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Cardinals">Cardinals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Cardinals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Cardinals_created_by_Sixtus_IV" title="Cardinals created by Sixtus IV">Cardinals created by Sixtus IV</a></div>
<p>Sixtus created an unusually large number of cardinals during his pontificate (23) who were drawn from the roster of the princely houses of Italy, France and Spain, thus ensuring that many of his policies continued after his death:
</p>
<div>

</td></tr></tbody></table></div>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Portrayals">Portrayals</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Pope_Sixtus_IV&amp;action=edit&amp;section=13" title="Edit section: Portrayals">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Pope Sixtus is portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Arthur_Grosser" title="Arthur Grosser">Arthur Grosser</a> in the short film <i><a href="/wiki/Assassin%27s_Creed:_Lineage" title="Assassin&#39;s Creed: Lineage">Assassin's Creed: Lineage</a></i>, a prequel to the video game <i><a href="/wiki/Assassin%27s_Creed_II" title="Assassin&#39;s Creed II">Assassin's Creed II</a></i>.
</p><p>Pope Sixtus is portrayed by <a href="/wiki/James_Faulkner_(actor)" title="James Faulkner (actor)">James Faulkner</a> in the historical fantasy <i><a href="/wiki/Da_Vinci%27s_Demons" title="Da Vinci&#39;s Demons">Da Vinci's Demons</a></i> as having an identical <a href="/wiki/Twin" title="Twin">twin</a>, Alessandro. Shortly after the true Pope Sixtus, Francesco, was elected on conclave, Alessandro usurped the Holy See and had his brother locked up in <a href="/wiki/Castel_Sant%27Angelo" title="Castel Sant&#39;Angelo">Castel Sant'Angelo</a>. The series implies that many of the more unsavoury parts of Sixtus' reign were really the work of his <a href="/wiki/Evil_twin" title="Evil twin">evil twin</a>, who was out to gain power for himself.
</p><p>Pope Sixtus is portrayed by <a href="/wiki/Raul_Bova" class="mw-redirect" title="Raul Bova">Raul Bova</a> in the second season, and <a href="/wiki/John_Lynch_(actor)" title="John Lynch (actor)">John Lynch</a> in the third season of the TV series <i><a href="/wiki/Medici:_Masters_of_Florence" class="mw-redirect" title="Medici: Masters of Florence">Medici: Masters of Florence</a></i>.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;29&#93;</a></sup>
</p>

<li id="cite_note-8"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-8">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">His most scandalous expenditures however occurred in relation to his reception of <a href="/wiki/Eleanor_of_Naples,_Duchess_of_Ferrara" title="Eleanor of Naples, Duchess of Ferrara">Eleonora of Aragon</a>. They reportedly involved expensive decorations, <a href="/wiki/Gilding" title="Gilding">gilded</a> <a href="/wiki/Chamber_pot" title="Chamber pot">chamber pots</a>, a feast with many courses ending with <a href="/wiki/Capon" title="Capon">capon</a> sculptures made from <a href="/wiki/Marzipan" title="Marzipan">marzipan</a>, three edible life-sized statues of <a href="/wiki/Hercules" title="Hercules">Hercules</a> below ones of <a href="/wiki/Bacchus" class="mw-redirect" title="Bacchus">Bacchus</a> and <a href="/wiki/Ariadne" title="Ariadne">Ariadne</a> made from <a href="/wiki/Candy" title="Candy">sweets</a> being served with desert.</span>
</li>
